## Human

now scrooge: The bot's poor performance stems from a fundamental oversight in risk management: **position sizing ignores spread costs**, leading to excessive risk per trade. Additionally, the decision to make the **OTR gate advisory** allows trades with unfavorable cost structures, compounding the problem. These two issues together ensure that even if the entry signals are valid, the net profitability is systematically eroded.

## 1. Position sizing fails to account for spread

In `main.py`, the sizing calculation uses only the price distance to the stop (gross risk) and ignores the spread cost that will be incurred on both entry and exit:

```python
risk_points = signal.risk_points                 # price distance to stop
pip_value = oanda.get_pip_value_per_unit(...)
risk_pips = risk_points / pip_size
pnl_per_unit = risk_pips * pip_value             # gross PnL per unit if stop is hit
units = (risk_amount / pnl_per_unit) * size_mult
```

**Consequence:**  
If the spread is 2 pips and the stop distance is 10 pips, the actual loss per unit is 12 pips (10 + 2×spread). The bot sizes as if the loss is only 10 pips, so the real risk taken is **20% larger than intended**. This over‑risking inflates drawdowns and can cause the daily loss limit to be hit earlier than expected.

**Fix:**  
Include the full spread cost in the risk per unit:
```python
spread_cost_per_unit = (live_spread / pip_size) * pip_value * 2
total_risk_per_unit = pnl_per_unit + spread_cost_per_unit
units = (risk_amount / total_risk_per_unit) * size_mult
```

## 2. OTR gate was demoted to advisory, allowing high‑cost trades

The `signal_engine` now always passes the OTR gate, even when the round‑trip spread consumes more than 25% of the half‑box (i.e. `otr > 0.25`). Such trades have a very low expected value because the spread eats most of the potential profit. For example, a trade with a 20‑pip half‑box and a 6‑pip round‑trip spread has an OTR of 30% – it is mathematically unlikely to be profitable after costs, regardless of the reversal pattern.

The original box theory implicitly assumed tight spreads; ignoring OTR in today’s forex environment lets through too many losing setups.

**Fix:**  
Reinstate the OTR gate as a hard filter – do not enter if `otr > otr_marginal` (e.g. 25%). The OTR threshold should be derived from historical win/loss analysis of each pair.

## 3. Sucker move detection may be too lenient

The recent change to a “flexible window” (allowing choppy moves) may admit setups that are not true explosive moves. The original definition required 3–5 consecutive directional candles with no meaningful pullback. The current algorithm counts directional candles within a window and only requires net movement in the correct direction. This can flag slow, grinding moves as sucker moves, which have lower reversal probability.

**Potential improvement:**  
Tighten the sucker move criteria: require at least 3 consecutive directional candles (ignoring small pullbacks) and a minimum speed (e.g., move > 1 ATR in the window). Monitor the win rate of trades triggered by “clean” vs. “choppy” sucker moves to calibrate.

## 4. Re‑entry cooldown after winning trades is too short

After a take‑profit hit (winning trade), the bot imposes only a 15‑minute cooldown. This often leads to re‑entering the same zone immediately, sometimes resulting in a second loss that erases the previous gain. A longer cooldown (e.g., 60 minutes) would give the zone time to reset and avoid machine‑gunning the same level.

## Summary of critical fixes

- **Correct position sizing** to include spread costs – this is the most urgent and impactful fix.
- **Reinstate the OTR gate** as a hard filter to prevent trades with poor cost‑to‑profit ratios.
- Review sucker move criteria and cooldown periods to reduce low‑probability re‑entries.

Without these changes, the bot will continue to behave no better than random, because its risk is mis‑measured and its trade selection includes too many inherently unprofitable setups.

**Calmar = Annualized Return / Max Drawdown**

The minimum useful sample is 3 months, ideally a year. With what we have:

## Claude

Calmar of 5 is achievable but requires controlling **both sides** of the ratio simultaneously. Here's what it takes:

**Calmar = Annualized Return / Max Drawdown ≥ 5**

If you target max drawdown of 5%, you need 25% annualized return. At 3% max drawdown, you only need 15% annualized — much more realistic for these bots.

The returns side is mostly strategy (gates, R:R, win rate — already working on that). The **drawdown side is directly controllable right now** via circuit breakers.

The two levers that most directly cap max drawdown:

1. **Daily loss limit** — stop new entries if the session is down more than X% of equity. Currently neither bot has this. A 1.5% daily limit would have prevented several of the bad multi-loss days.

2. **Equity high-water mark drawdown limit** — track the peak equity ever reached, pause if current equity drops more than Y% below it (e.g. 3%). This directly caps the Calmar denominator.

3. **Reduce `max_trade_risk_pct`** — Scrooge is at 1% per trade. At 5 concurrent positions that's 5% theoretical max simultaneous loss. Dropping to 0.65% per trade with max 4 positions limits single-session wipeout.

Which of those do you want me to implement, and do you want them on both bots or just one? The HWM drawdown limit is the most powerful for Calmar specifically — it directly prevents the denominator from growing.

## Human

whats my best way to maximize my compounded growth

---

## Claude

The mathematical answer is the **Kelly Criterion** — it's the only formula proven to maximize long-run compounded growth (geometric mean).

**Kelly fraction: `f* = (R·p - q) / R`**

Where R = reward/risk ratio, p = win rate, q = 1-p.

Example at p=50%, R=1.75:
`f* = (1.75×0.5 - 0.5) / 1.75 = 21.4%` risk per trade

But there are two problems applying this right now:

**Problem 1 — You don't have reliable edge estimates yet**
15-25 trades gives wild variance on p and R estimates. If your true win rate is 45% instead of 50%, Kelly says risk 8% not 21%. Overestimating edge and sizing full Kelly is the fastest way to blow up.

**Problem 2 — Your Scrooge positions are correlated**
Kelly assumes independent bets. You have AUD_JPY, AUD_CHF, EUR_AUD all open simultaneously — when AUD moves, they all move together. That's effectively one large bet, not five small ones. Correlated positions inflate drawdowns without increasing expected return, which is the worst possible outcome for compounding.

---

**What actually maximizes compounding in your situation:**

1. **You're already auto-compounding** — both bots size on % of equity, so profits compound automatically. This is correct.

2. **Drawdown protection is compounding protection.** A 20% drawdown requires 25% to recover. A 50% drawdown requires 100%. The Calmar circuit breakers we discussed directly protect compounding.

3. **Get to 100+ trades before adjusting size.** Right now 1% per trade is appropriate — conservative enough to survive the learning period, not so small it doesn't matter.

4. **Reduce Scrooge's position correlation.** Cap at one position per currency (no two AUD pairs, no two JPY pairs open simultaneously). This is the single biggest structural improvement for compounding — it turns 5 correlated bets into 5 independent ones, which is what Kelly actually assumes.

5. **Once you have 100+ trades**, calculate your realized p and R, apply half-Kelly (f*/2) as your risk %, and let it ride. Half-Kelly delivers ~75% of maximum growth with ~25% of the variance.

The short answer: protect against big drawdowns, eliminate correlation, collect 100 trades, then size to half-Kelly. That's the path to a compounding curve that doesn't get interrupted by recoveries.

Want me to add the correlation cap to Scrooge (one position per currency) and the daily drawdown circuit breaker to both bots?

## Claude

It does have a ratcheting SL — three stages:

1. **Stage 1** — Move SL to break-even when price is 50% of the way to TP1
2. **Stage 2** — Move SL to TP1 level when price crosses TP1 (locks TP1 profit)
3. **Stage 3** — Hybrid trail: `max(hourly candle low, entry + 80% × peak gain, peak - 1.5×ATR)` — whichever gives the tightest floor

The problem is none of the current 5 positions have reached Stage 1 yet — they opened, went against you, and have been sitting underwater since. The ratchet only activates once price moves 50% toward TP1, which hasn't happened on any of them.

So the mechanism exists and is correct — the positions just never got far enough in the green to trigger it. The underlying issue is the entries themselves were poor (entered into what became a sustained move against them), not the profit-locking logic.
